Technical Report MPI-SWS-2021-004 November 4, 2021 Pirouette: Higher-Order Typed Functional Choreographies Journal: PACMPL Volume: MPI-SWS Tech Report 11
Andrew K. Hirsch, Deepak Garg

Abstract
. We present Pirouette, a language for typed higher-order functional choreographic programming. Pirouette offers programmers the ability to write a centralized functional program and compile it via endpoint projection into programs for each node in a distributed system. Moreover, Pirouette is defined generically over a (local) language of messages, and lifts guarantees about the message type system to its own. Message type soundness also guarantees deadlock freedom. All of our results are verified in Coq.



1

1.
Introduction

Higher-order typed functional programming has proven to be a powerful technique for writing single-machine programs.
It allows for strong guarantees through types along with code reuse through higher-order programming.
However, currently, writing
distributed
programs using functional programming requires writing separate code for each node in the distributed system, then using
send
and
receive
expressions to transmit data between nodes.
This makes it easy to write code that
deadlocks
, or gets stuck because patterns of
send
s and
receive
s do not match.

Session types

(
Scalas and
Yoshida 2019
;
Wadler 2012
;
DeYoung et al. 2012
;
Dardha
et al. 2012
;
Toninho
et al. 2012
;
Carbone
et al. 2007
)
offer one solution.
Session types describe the pattern of
send
s and
receive
s in a program, allowing a compiler to catch the possibility of deadlock.
However, session types are complicated to work with, and the programmer is still left trying to match up
send
and
receive
patterns by hand.

Choreographic programming

(
Montesi 2020
;
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017a
;
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017b
;
Montesi 2013
;
Lanese
et al. 2013
;
Dalla Preda et al. 2015
)
offers another solution.
This is a programming paradigm that writes the distributed program as a single program, ensuring that
send
s and
receive
s match by combining
send
and
receive
into one construct.
Choreographic languages guarantee
deadlock freedom by design
, so the programmer gets strong guarantees on the communication patterns in their program.
Until now, however, choreographic programming forced the user into a lower-order, imperative, and un(i)typed universe.
This paper presents Pirouette, the first language for choreographic programming which is also higher-order, functional, and typed.

Consider a standard example: the bookseller protocol.
In this protocol, a buyer is looking to buy a book, so they send the book’s title to a bookseller.
The bookseller returns its price, and the buyer checks if that price is within their budget.
If they can afford the book, they inform the bookseller who tells them a delivery date for their book; otherwise, they tell the seller they will not buy the book.
After this, the protocol ends.

To write this program with session types, we first create a type describing the interactions from both points of view.
That is, we create a type for the buyer that includes “send the title to the seller, and then receive back a price” along with a (dual) type for the seller that includes “receive the title from the buyer, then send them the price.”
First, we must ensure that the two types match: when the buyer sends to the seller, the seller receives from the buyer.
These types do not describe details like “if the book is within budget, tell the seller yes.”
Instead, it says “either send the seller ‘yes’ and receive a delivery date, or send them ‘no’ and end the protocol”.
We then write a program for the buyer and another for the seller, which contain all the nasty details, and a type-checker ensures that these programs have the correct communication patterns.

In contrast, a choreographic programmer would write a single program, and expect the language and compiler to ensure that the resulting programs for the buyer and bookseller are deadlock free.
For instance, such a programmer might write the following:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{book\_title}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.b;\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.\textsf{prices}[b]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.p;\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.(p<\textsf{budget})\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}};\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.\textsf{get\_delivery\_date}(b)\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{d\_date};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{(Some d\_date)}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{None}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Note that the choreography is a program, not merely the type of another program.
In this program,
Buyer
sends a string
book_title
to
Seller
, who binds that string to the variable
b
.

Seller
then sends a message to
Buyer
, which is the result of looking up the price of the book in a map that
Seller
holds.
Note that this message is written in a separate programming language, which defines the map-lookup syntax.
Once
Buyer
has the price, they decide whether or not to buy based on their budget.
Either way, they inform
Seller
whether they took the left (
L
) or the right (
R
) branch.
In the left branch,
Seller
calls some function in the local language to get the delivery date.
(We use the syntax
f(x)
for function calls in the local syntax to emphasize that these are function calls in the local language, whereas Pirouette uses the syntax
F\mkern 5.0muX
for its function calls.)

Seller
transmits the result of this function call to
Buyer
, who returns the date, wrapped so that the entire program returns a value on buyer of type
option

date
.
In the right branch,
Buyer
simply returns
None
.

Note that communication is always matched up: sends and receives are combined, which guarantees deadlock freedom.
The return value on
Buyer
makes this closer to functional programming than previous work on choreographies.
Note that choreographies returning values and having standard simple types (i.e., not session types) are both innovations of this work.

Choreographies excel when more than two parties must interact.
For instance,
Carbone and
Montesi 2013
suggest a change to the bookseller protocol similar to the following:
Seller
sends the price to two buyers who want to share the purchase of a book,
\textsf{Buyer}_{1}
and
\textsf{Buyer}_{2}
.

\textsf{Buyer}_{2}
then tells
\textsf{Buyer}_{1}
how much they are willing to contribute to the purchase, who then responds to
Seller
as before.
We can write a modified choreography as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{1}}.\textsf{book\_title}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.b;\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.\textsf{prices}[b]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{1}}.p;\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.\textsf{prices}[b]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{2}}.p;\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{2}}.(p/2)\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{1}}.\textsf{contrib};\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{1}}.(p-\textsf{contrib}<\textsf{budget})\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}};\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.\textsf{get\_delivery\_date}(b)\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{1}}.\textsf{d\_date};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{1}}.\textsf{(Some d\_date)}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{1}}.\textsf{None}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

In order to use session types to describe this communication pattern, we must use
multiparty session types
, a major increase in complexity
(
Scalas and
Yoshida 2019
;
Carbone
et al. 2007
)
.
However, choreographies handle this without difficulty.

The choreographies explained so far can be expressed in prior work.
This paper introduces
functional choreographies
.
To understand the need for these, notice how we can abstract out a pattern from our two protocols.
A buyer (say
Buyer
) sends a book title to
Seller
, who looks up its price.
There is then some process, possibly involving communications, which results in a decision by
Buyer
, who then informs
Seller
of that decision.
If
Buyer
decides to buy the book, they get back a delivery date, otherwise they get nothing.
We can modify the choreography so that the decision is its own function as follows:

\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{Bookseller}(F)\mathrel{\coloneqq}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{book\_title}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.b;\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}let}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{decision}\mathrel{\coloneqq}F\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.(\textsf{prices}[b])\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}in}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{decision}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}};\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.\textsf{get\_delivery\_date}(b)\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{the\_date};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{(Some the\_date)}\end{array}\end{array}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{None}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Here,
F
is a function with a choreographic body which takes a price on
Seller
as input and outputs a Boolean on
Buyer
.
We can implement either of the previous protocols by changing
F
:

\frac{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.p)\mathrel{\coloneqq}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.p\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.p;\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.(p<\textsf{budget})\end{array}}\frac{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.p)\mathrel{\coloneqq}\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.p\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.p;\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}.p\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{2}}.p;\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}_{2}}.(p/2)\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{contrib};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.(p-\textsf{contrib}<\textsf{budget})\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}}

Moreover, we can give
F
a type which enforces that it takes a price located on
Seller
to a Boolean value on
Buyer
, as desired.
We write this type
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}price}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\textsf{@}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}bool}}
.
Then we can give
Bookseller
the type
(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}price}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\textsf{@}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Seller}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}bool}})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}date}}
, indicating that it is a higher-order function which expects an input that abstracts the decision in the bookseller example.
This illustrates the programming convenience gained by combining higher-order functional programming with the choreographic-programming paradigm.
For instance, previous works on choreographies could not treat choreographic functions as data, preventing this form of abstraction
(
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017b
)
.

Contributions

As stated before, Pirouette is the first (higher-order) choreographic functional programming language.
Pirouette features simple located types of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{Buyer}}.\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}bool}}
and familiar type constructs like function spaces.
Following the choreography philosophy, Pirouette guarantees deadlock freedom by design, without the use of complicated session types.
Previously, higher-order choreographic programming was only supported through the informal development in Choral
(
Giallorenzo et al. 2020
)
, which is useful despite offering no guarantees.
In the following, we point out some salient technical contributions of our design.

First, Pirouette is
generic
in the language of messages.
Note that, sometimes, locations send non-atomic messages like
p/2
, which can be arbitrary expressions.
In previous work, messages were either from a particular (and very simple) language
(
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017a
)
or were assumed to compute to a value in finite time
(
Carbone and
Montesi 2013
)
.
Pirouette is defined generically on top of
any
message language (which we refer to as the
local
language), with very few syntactic constraints.
We further show that a sound type system for the expression language can be lifted to the level of choreographies.

Two key features of choreographies are
out-of-order execution
and
endpoint projection
.
We can think of a choreography as an arbitrary interleaving of communication between programs running at different locations.
However, programs with the same communication pattern can have other interleavings as well.
Choreographies, despite
syntactically
being one arbitrary interleaving are able to
semantically
represent all interleavings by allowing out-of-order execution.
We follow previous work by defining an equivalence relation
\equiv
on choreographies to reason about out-of-order execution.
However, unlike previous work, we are able to show that defining our operational semantics by appealing to
\equiv
gives a weaker-than-desired semantics.
Pirouette’s semantics provides a stronger form of out-of-order execution via a novel combination of a labeled-transition system and a
block set
, which guarantees that out-of-order execution does not violate causality.

Endpoint projection formalizes the intuition of a choreography as representing a collection of programs running at different locations by extracting a program for each location (later, we use the term
control program
for the projected program at each location).
This justifies choreographies as a way of writing distributed programs, and allows us to state and prove that Pirouette programs are deadlock free.
We follow a new design principle for choreographies:
equivalence begets equality
.
That is, equivalent choreographies always project to exactly the same program for each location.
In previous work, equivalence was used more liberally which prevented such a clean theorem.
For instance,
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017a
use equivalence to reason about recursion unfolding, so equivalent programs may project to programs where unfolding has and has not been applied.

Moreover, somewhat surprisingly, we show that deadlock freedom is a corollary to the soundness of our type system as well as the soundness and completeness of endpoint projection.
Previous work was able to take advantage of the assumption that messages always produce a value along with the lower-order nature of their choreographies.
Because our choreographies are higher-order and our messages are not assumed to compute, we have to appeal to the soundness of our type system to ensure that our choreographies are always able to take a step.

We have formalized our entire development in Coq and mechanically verified proofs of all of our theorems.
As mentioned by
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2019
, there have been several instances of flaws found in proofs of major theorems in concurrency theory in recent years.
Therefore, in order to show that Pirouette’s guarantees about deadlock freedom are trustworthy, we formalize our arguments.
In soon-to-be-published work, a small choreography language has been formalized along with its endpoint-projection operation
(
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021c
;
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021b
)
.
However, Pirouette is a much more substantial language than was formalized in that work.

To summarize, we make the following contributions:

•

We introduce Pirouette, the first functional choreography language.
We present its operational semantics and a (simple) type system.
The operational semantics allow for out-of-order execution, mimicking the execution of a distributed program.
These semantics are based on a novel idea of blocking sets – locations that are blocked on other operations and cannot reduce (Section
3
).

•

We describe a general set of constraints on the local (message) language, allowing almost any expression-based language to be used as the local language (Section
2
).
Type soundness for functional choreographies lifts from type soundness for the local language (Section
3.2
).

•

We study equivalence for functional choreographies, which allows for reasoning about out-of-order execution.
We show that defining out-of-order execution based on this equivalence leads to a weaker-than-desired operational semantics when local reduction is allowed (Section
4
).

•

We show how endpoint projection extracts programs with explicit send and receive constructs from functional choreographies.
This translation is sound and complete.
Moreover, we show that well-typed, projected systems are deadlock free by design, and that this deadlock freedom follows from the soundness of the type system and the soundness and completeness of endpoint projection (Section
5
).

•

All our metatheory has been formalized in Coq and all our theorems have been mechanically verified.
We discuss the particularities of our Coq implementation in Section
6
.

Concurrent, independent work
(
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021a
)
also explores higher-order functional choreographies.
However, they have a very different design philosophy, which leads to a very different technical setup.
For more comparison, see the discussion of related work in Section
7
.

2.
System Model

We begin by discussing the assumptions we make about the system that Pirouette programs run on.
We have made these assumptions as lightweight as possible.
In particular, we assume that the system consists of a collection of nodes, each of which can run local programs and which can send and receive messages from other nodes.
We also allow local programs to be typed, so that we can lift the type system to the choreography level.

2.1.
Locations

We assume a set
\mathcal{L}
of
locations
, which we write as
\ell
,
\ell_{1}
,
\ell_{2}
, and so on.
These names are treated atomically, so we do not assume any additional operations on locations.
However, we do assume that location equality is decidable, so we can distinguish different locations.

Intuitively, locations refer to nodes in a distributed system.
However, it’s worth noting that there is nothing that prevents a node from being a thread, or a process, or any other entity that can run Turing-complete programs and send and receive messages.

2.2.
Communication

We assume that every location can communicate with every other location synchronously.
That is, if
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
sends a message to
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
does not continue until
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
has received the message, and then
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
may continue.
Message sending is instantaneous and certain: messages do not get “lost in the air.”

Nodes should be able to send and receive two kinds of messages: values of local programs (described below) and two special
synchronization messages
, written
L
and
R
.
These will be used in the choreography language to ensure that different locations stay in lock-step with each other.

We also require that each node be able to run a functional
control
program which can send and receive messages, while also running programs in the local language.
We describe the precise requirements in Section
5.1
, when we have the necessary background.

2.3.
Local Programs

We assume that every node runs programs in a
local
expression-based language.
Our design treats this language generically, requiring only that it allows certain operations and equations.

Our first requirement is that expressions include
variables
.
We model messages as values, and receipt as binding a value to a variable.
We implement variable binding via substitution, which we write
e_{1}[x\mapsto e_{2}]
.
Substitution must satisfy three standard equations:

•

x[x\mapsto e]=e

•

e[x\mapsto x]=e

•

e_{1}[x\mapsto e_{2}][y\mapsto e_{3}]=e_{1}[y\mapsto e_{3}][x\mapsto(e_{2}[y\mapsto e_{3}])]
whenever
x\not\in\text{FV}(e_{3})

We require a function
FV(e)
which returns the set of free variables in
e
.
We require that if
x\not\in\text{FV}(e_{1})
, then
e_{1}[x\mapsto e_{2}]=e_{1}
.

We only send values as messages, so we assume a predicate
\text{Value}(e)
which determines whether
e
is a value.
We require two special values,
true
and
false
, which we use for branching in choreographies.
We additionally require that all values are closed, in order to allow them to be sent.
To see why, imagine that we send some open expression
e
from
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
.
Since
e
is open,
e
contains some free variable
x
, which refers to some data on
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
.
However, when we send
e
to
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
, this information is lost and
x
might be captured by a binder in
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
’s program.

Finally, we require that an operational semantics be defined relationally for local expressions.
We write
e_{1}\mathrel{\Rightarrow_{\textrm{e}}}e_{2}
to denote that
e_{1}
steps to
e_{2}
in the operational semantics.
The only requirement on this semantics is that values do not take steps: if
\text{Value}(v)
, then
v\mathrel{\not\Rightarrow_{\textrm{e}}}e
for any
e
.

2.3.1.
Examples

Simply-typed functional languages easily satisfy the requirements above.
However, any expression-based language can be used, not only ones which define functions.
We discuss two examples here.

Example 1 (Call-by-Value
\lambda
-Calculus).

The call-by-value
\lambda
-calculus, extended with recursive functions, Boolean values and if-then-else expression, almost fits our requirements.
However, we must restrict values to be closed.

Example 2 (A Natural-Number Language).

We provide a language with the following syntax:

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\ignorespaces\textrm{Expressions}&e&::=&x\,\mid\,0\,\mid\,S\,e\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}true}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}false}}\end{array}

Intuitively,
0
stands for
0
as a natural number,
S
is the successor operation on natural numbers, and
true
and
false
stand for Boolean truth and falsity, respectively.
Since there are no binders, substitution is easy to define.
Any closed term is a value.

This is similar to the language of messages in
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017a
, but modified to fit our requirements.
In particular, we (a) allow more than one variable, which is treated via substitution instead of as a reference to state, and (b) add the
true
and
false
terms.

We include this language to demonstrate that our requirements do not force the choice of
\lambda
-calculus as an expression language.
Indeed, we will see later that we can equip this language with a type system which results in a sound choreographic type system.

2.4.
Typed Local Programs

The guarantees we provide for Pirouette depend on the guarantees provided by the local language’s type system.
If we do not consider the local type system, then we are able to provide a sound and complete translation to a language with explicit send and receive constructs.
If the local type system guarantees preservation, but not progress (as in a unityped system), then we are also able to prove preservation of the choreographic type system.
Finally, soundness of the local type system implies not only type soundness for Pirouette, but also deadlock freedom.

We allow the types for the local language to be any language of simple types.
We require that
true
and
false
have the same type, which we refer to as
bool
.
Note, however, that this type may not be called
bool
, as we will see in Example
5
.

We assume that the type system can be presented in terms of a judgment
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}
, where
\Gamma
is a sequence of variable-type pairs written
x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}
.
We additionally require that typing be unique: if
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}}
.
We only use this requirement to show that types play well with equational reasoning (Theorem
3
).
While this requirement is unusual, we note that it is usually satisfied by simple-type systems.
We conjecture that this requirement could be removed with a small change to our choreographic language; we return to this point in Section
4
.

The other requirements can all be framed as admissible rules; those rules can be found in Figure
1
.
Since these rules should be admissible, we do
not
require that these are actual rules in the type system; merely that we can use them to build typing proofs.
This will be important when building typing proofs for choreographies on top of typing proofs for the local language.

\frac{}{\Gamma,x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}\mathrel{\vdash}x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}}\quad\textsc{Var}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}true}}\mathrel{:}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}bool}}}\quad\textsc{True}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}false}}\mathrel{:}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}bool}}}\quad\textsc{False}\\
\frac{\Gamma,x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}},y\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}}\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{3}}}{\Gamma,y\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}},x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{3}}}\quad\textsc{Exchange}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}}{\Gamma,y\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}}\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}}\quad\textsc{Weakening}\\
\frac{\Gamma,x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}\quad x\notin\text{FV}(e)}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}}\quad\textsc{Strengthening}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e_{1}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}\quad\Gamma,x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}\mathrel{\vdash}e_{2}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e_{2}[x\mapsto e_{1}]\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}}}\quad\textsc{Substitution}

Figure 1.
Required-Admissible Rules for Local Type Systems

First, we require that variables be typed according to the context, and that
true
and
false
be typed in the
bool
type.
We also require that the standard structural rules of
Exchange
and
Weakening
be allowed.
The
Strengthening
rule is unusually-presented, but is common in most type systems: non-free variables can be safely removed from the typing context.
Finally, we require the standard property of
Substitution
: substituting a well-typed variable into a well-typed expression yields a well-typed expression.

Sound Type Systems

We say that a local type system is
sound
if it additionally satisfies the following three requirements:

•

(
Boolean Invertibility
) The type
bool
is invertible for values: if
v
is a value and
\mathrel{\vdash}v\mathrel{:}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}bool}}
then either
v=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}true}}
or
v=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}false}}
.

•

(
Preservation
) If
e_{1}\mathrel{\Rightarrow_{\textrm{e}}}e_{2}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e_{1}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}
, then
\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e_{2}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}
.

•

(
Progress
) If
e_{1}
is closed and
\mathrel{\vdash}e_{1}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}
, then either
e_{1}
is a value or there is an
e_{2}
such that
e_{1}\mathrel{\Rightarrow_{\textrm{e}}}e_{2}
.

2.4.1.
Examples

Each of the languages that served as examples above can be given type systems that satisfy the requirements above.
In fact, we can give the
\lambda
-calculus two type systems, though one is not sound.

Example 3 (Simply-Typed
\lambda
-Calculus).

This is the paradigmatic example of a typed local language.
None of the rules in Figure
1
are difficult; most of the proofs are completely standard.
Moreover, it is sound: the progress and preservation proofs are standard, and the invertability proof is easy.

Example 4 (Typed Natural-Numbers).

We use two types:
int
and
bool
.
Then, we have the following rules:

\frac{x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}\in\Gamma}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}x\mathrel{:}\tau}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}0\mathrel{:}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}int}}}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}t\mathrel{:}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}int}}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}S\,t\mathrel{:}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}int}}}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}true}}\mathrel{:}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}bool}}}\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}false}}\mathrel{:}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}bool}}}

This makes the rules in Figure
1
easy to prove, and soundness is trivial.

Example 5 (Unityped
\lambda
-Calculus).

Using the idea of “untyped is unityped”, we develop a type system for
\lambda
-calculus with only one type,
\ast
, and only one typing rule:

\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}\ast}\quad\textsc{Trivial}

Here, we satisfy the requirement to have a type for Boolean values by setting
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}bool}}=\ast
.
The rules in Figure
1
are trivial, as is
Preservation
.
However, this system is
not
sound:
bool
is not invertible, and uni-typed
\lambda
-calculus does not satisfy progress (programs may get stuck).

3.
Functional Choreographies

We introduce Pirouette, our language for writing distributed programs in a functional, choreographic style.
We guarantee deadlock freedom along with the standard static guarantees of simple-type systems.
Moreover, we allow higher-order computations by allowing choreographies to be passed to other choreographies as inputs.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\ignorespaces\textrm{Locations}&{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathcal{L}\\
\textrm{Synchronization Labels}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}&::=&{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\\
\textrm{Choreographies}&C&::=&X\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0muC\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}let}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{\coloneqq}C_{1}\mathrel{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}in}}}C_{2}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C\\
&&\,\mid&C\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e\,\mid\,C_{1}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\end{array}

Figure 2.
Functional Choreographies Syntax

The syntax of Pirouette can be found in Figure
2
.
Note that choreographies contain two types of variables: choreography variables, which stand for the result of a distributed computation (i.e., a choreography), and local variables which are the variables of the local language seen in Section
2
.
We write choreography variables in upper case (
X
,
Y
, etc.) and choreography variables in lower case (
x
,
y
, etc.).

Every location has its own namespace of local variables, so
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.x\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x
.
This is reflected in the defintion of substitution: substitution of choreography variables has the standard form
C_{1}[X\mapsto C_{2}]
,
while substitution of local variables has the form
C[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern-6.0mu\mid\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto e]
.
The first notion of substitution is standard.
The second walks through a term looking for a subterm of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e^{\prime}
, and then replaces
e^{\prime}
with
e^{\prime}[x\mapsto e]
.
(Note that the location name
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
must be the same as the one in the substitution!)

We write
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e
to represent the choreography that returns the result of running
e
on
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
.
To use this value in a future choreography, we use the syntax
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}let}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{\coloneqq}C_{1}\mathrel{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}in}}}C_{2}
.
This runs
C_{1}
until it returns a value on
\ell
, and then binds that value to
x
under
\ell
in
C_{2}
.

We write
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC
to represent
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
evaluating
e
and then sending the resulting value
v
to
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
.
The variable
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x
is bound to
v
in the continuation choreography
C
, representing
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
’s receipt of the message.

The distributed program can branch based on the result of a test on a local machine.
We write this
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}
.
However, this can quickly break causality.
To see why, consider the program

\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.3\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.4
.
In this program,
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
behaves differently depending on the behavior of a test performed on
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
, but
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
has never been told about the result of that test!
In order to fix this problem, we require that
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
inform
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
of which branch was taken before
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
can behave differently in the two branches.
We do this using the syntax
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0muC
.
Here
d
can either be
L
or
R
, where
L
represents taking the then branch, while
R
represents taking the else branch.
Thus, we can safely write the following program:

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.3\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.4\end{array}

Note that this is only required because
\ell_{2}
’s behavior differs
in the two branches.
If
\ell_{2}
behaves the same, no synchronization is required.
Thus, the following program is okay:

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x+2\end{array}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x+3\end{array}\end{array}

Because it behaves the same in both branches,
\ell_{3}
never needs to be informed about which branch is taken, even though it appears in the branches.
Moreover,
\ell_{2}
only needs to be informed about which branch is taken after it receives its message from
\ell_{3}
.

There are two types of functions available in Pirouette, local and global functions.
Local functions expect a local value as input, stored on some particular node, whereas global functions expect a choreography as input.
Both types of functions may be recursive.
We write
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C
for the (recursive) function named
F
which expects a local value on
\ell
as input, and has body
C
.
We write
C\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e
for the application of function
C
to input
e
which is stored on
\ell
.
For the global function named
F
which takes an input named
X
and has body
C
, we write
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C
.
As is traditional, we write
C_{1}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}
for the application of function
C_{1}
to
C_{2}
.

We adopt a call-by-value semantics, so we evaluate inputs to values before applying functions.
As described in Section
2
, local values are defined by the local language and are always closed.
Choreography values are programs of any of the forms: (a)
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.v
(where
v
is a local value), (b)
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C
(where the only free variables in
C
are
F
and
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x
), or (c)
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C
(where the only free variables in
C
are
F
and
X
).

We define an operation
\textsf{LN}\left(C\right)
which collects all of the location names in
C
in a set.
We write
\text{FCV}(C)
for the free choreography variables in
C
, and
\text{FEV}(C)
for the collection of free expression variables in
C
, tagged with the locations that own them.
We write
\text{FEV}_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}(C)
for the collection of expression variables free under
\ell
in C.

3.1.
Operational Semantics

Intuitively, if two locations
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
both take actions, they should be able to do this in either order.
For instance, consider the Pirouette program

{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.2+3\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.3*4\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.y;\mkern 5.0muC
.
Here,
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{3}
are both working on computations whose results they expect to send to
\ell_{2}
.
Since these are different locations, both should be able to work on their programs at the same time.
Thus in the semantics we should be able to reduce this program to either

{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.5\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.3*4\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.y;\mkern 5.0muC
or
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.2+3\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.12\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.y;\mkern 5.0muC
,
performing the local reductions in either order.
However,
\ell_{2}
is just one location, so it cannot listen for two messages at once.
Thus, even if we reduce the above program to

{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.5\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.12\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.y;\mkern 5.0muC

we are forced to reduce the send from
\ell_{1}
before the send from
\ell_{3}
, since the second is waiting on
\ell_{2}
.

In order to allow for this behavior, we keep track of a set of
blocked
locations in our operational semantics.
Intuitively, blocked locations cannot take a step.
By keeping track of what locations are blocked, we can allow out-of-order-execution among non-blocked locations.

However, this is not quite enough to get the behavior we want.
Consider this program, which represents a system where
\ell_{1}
branches on
e
and then does nothing, while
\ell_{2}
returns the result of
3+5
independent of
\ell_{1}
’s choice:
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.3+5\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.3+5
.
Here,
\ell_{2}
ought to be able to make progress, reducing this program to
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.8\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.8
.
Note that this progress was atomic.
Thus, it would be illegitimate to reduce different programs in the two branches.
For instance, reducing

\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.3+5\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.3*4
to
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.8\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.12

requires reducing different programs in the two branches, whereas, in reality, only one of those two programs should be reduced and that program should be chosen by
\ell_{1}
.

In order to prevent this type of reduction, we track
what
reduction is happening, resulting in a labeled transition system.
We refer to labels in Pirouette as “redices,” and to a single label as a “redex.”
In the second-to-last example above, we note that in each branch, we reduce a 3+5 to 8 on
\ell_{2}
.
Since we are doing the same reduction in each branch, we can reduce the whole program.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\notin B\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\quad e_{1}\mathrel{\Rightarrow_{\textrm{e}}}e_{2}}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC\mathrel{\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(e_{1}\Rightarrow e_{2})\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{2}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC}\quad\textsc{SendE}\frac{C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B\mathrel{\cup}\{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC_{2}}\quad\textsc{SendI}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\notin B\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\notin B\quad\text{Value}(v)\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.v\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC\mathrel{\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(v\mkern 3.0mu\text{value})\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\mkern-6.0mu\mid\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto v]}\quad\textsc{SendV}\\
\frac{C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{1}^{\prime}\quad C_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}^{\prime}}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{1}^{\prime}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{IfI}\\
\frac{C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell},{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0muC_{2}}\quad\textsc{SyncI}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\notin B\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\notin B\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0muC\mathrel{\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C}\quad\textsc{Sync}\\
\frac{C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{1}^{\prime}}{C_{1}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\text{Fun}(R)\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{1}^{\prime}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}}\quad\textsc{AppGlobalFun}\frac{C_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}^{\prime}}{C_{1}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\text{Arg}(R)\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{1}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{AppGlobalArg}\frac{\text{Value}(V)}{(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C)\mkern 5.0muV\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\text{GlobalFun}\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C[X\mapsto V,F\mapsto\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C]}\quad\textsc{AppGlobal}

Figure 3.
Selected Choreography Operational Semantics

Selected rules from the operational semantics can be found in Figure
3
.
The full rules, along with the syntax of redices, can be found in Appendix
A.2
.

Each rule has the form
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
, where
R
is a redex,
B
is a set of locations, and
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are choreographies.
Intuitively, this says that
C_{1}
can reduce to
C_{2}
using redex
R
even if every location in
B
is blocked.
With this interpretation, the rule
SendE
allows a location to reduce a message to a value before sending it.
The notation
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(e_{1}\Rightarrow e_{2})\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
is the redex for this reduction rule.
Note that we check both that
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\notin B
and that
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
in the premise of this rule.
The first check ensures that
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
is not blocked, since the reduction happens at that location.
The second check reflects the fact that sends from a node to itself is not meaningful.

The rule
SendI
allows reductions under a send construct, but only when the reduction can take place with both the sender and the receiver blocked.
This formalizes the intuition that while some locations are waiting, other locations can take actions.
Sending is formalized with the rule
SendV
.
This removes a send entirely.
Note that in the substitution,
\ell_{2}
’s variable is substituted with the message.
This formalizes the intuition that sends are modeled by binding the message to a variable in the receiver’s program.

The
IfI
rule is the only rule that makes use of redices.
By ensuring that the same redex is reduced on each branch of the if, we make sure that the only reductions that can be made are those which are invariant under which branch is taken.
Note that we again ensure that
\ell_{1}
is not taking an action in this reduction step, since it is currently occupied with the if itself.

The rules
SyncI
and
Sync
demonstrate the rather subtle effect that
Sync
has on the semantics.
The first rule demonstrates that synchronization induces blocking on the relevant locations.
However,
Sync
shows that this is the only effect that it has on the semantics of the choreography.

Finally, the rules
AppGlobalFun
,
AppGlobalArg
, and
AppGlobal
demonstrate how functions are treated.
Both the function and its argument can be evaluated in any order, but the redex is tagged with which choice is made.
This ensures that if statements don’t reduce a function in one branch and its argument in the other.
Finally, the
AppGlobal
rule shows that the semantics of a function is given as standard, with a parallel substitution.
However, we require that there be no blockers.
This comes from the fact that all of the locations work together to reduce functions at the same time.

3.2.
Type System

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\ignorespaces\textrm{Choreography Types}&{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}&::=&{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\textsf{@}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}\\
\textrm{Local Contexts}&\Gamma&::=&\cdot\,\mid\,\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}\\
\textrm{Choreography Contexts}&\Delta&::=&\cdot\,\mid\,\Delta,X\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}\end{array}

\frac{\Gamma\mathord{|}_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}\quad\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}};\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}}\quad\textsc{Send}\frac{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C_{1}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}\quad\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}};\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C_{2}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}}{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}let}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{\coloneqq}C_{1}\mathrel{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}in}}}C_{2}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}}\quad\textsc{DefLocal}\frac{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}};\mkern 5.0mu\Delta,F\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\textsf{@}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}}{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\textsf{@}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}}\quad\textsc{FunLocal}\frac{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta,F\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}},X\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}}{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}}\quad\textsc{FunGlobal}

Figure 4.
Pirouette Types (Syntax and Selected Rules)

Assuming that the local expression language has a type system as described in Section
2.4
, we can develop a language of types for Pirouette.
The syntax of Pirouette types can be found in Figure
4
, along with selected typing rules.
The full typing rules are in Appendix
B
.

There are three categories of Pirouette types, corresponding to the three categories of choreographic values.
The first category is a local type at some location, which we write
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}
.
This is the type given to values of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.v
.
Then there is the type of local functions, which we write
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\textsf{@}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}
.
Here,
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}
is an expression type, corresponding to the type of the input to the function, which is expected to be located on
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
.
The function then returns a
{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}
, which is a choreography type.
Finally, there is the type of global functions which take an arbitrary choreographic type as an input.
Thus, we write
{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\rightarrow}}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}
are both Pirouette types.

The choreographic type judgment is of the form
\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}
where
\Gamma
is a local context,
\Delta
is a global context,
C
is a choreography, and
{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}
is a choreographic type.
Global contexts are normal typing contexts relating choreography variables to choreography types.
However, local contexts have to relate variables to types, but must also keep track of the location of the variable.
Since each location is its own namespace, we relate variables paired with locations to local types (see the syntax of
\Gamma
in Figure
4
).

For any location
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
, we can recursively
project
the typing context of the location from a local context
\Gamma
as follows:

\Gamma\mathord{|}_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\cdot&\text{if}~\Gamma=\cdot\\
\Gamma^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}},x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}&\text{if}~\Gamma=\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}\\
\Gamma^{\prime}\mathord{|}_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}&\text{if}~\Gamma=\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}~\text{where}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\end{array}\right.

Intuitively, this gives the context for the namespace
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
.

We can see projection in action in the
Send
rule.
Here, we check that
e
has the local type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}
at the location
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
.
We then check the remainder of the choreography under the assumption that
x
has type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}

at
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
, since after the send
x
will be bound to the result of evaluating
e
.
Note that this works because values are closed; otherwise,
v
might not typecheck in
\ell_{2}
’s namespace.

The rule
DefLocal
shows how the program
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}let}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{\coloneqq}C_{1}\mathrel{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}in}}}C_{2}
acts as an elimination rule for the type
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}
.
We ensure that
C_{1}
has the type
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}
, and then we bind
x
to
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}
locally in
C_{2}
.
Finally,
FunLocal
and
FunGlobal
produce local and global function types, respectively.
Both also bind the function name to the appropriate function type, allowing for recursive function definitions.

The choreographic type system enjoys progress and preservation if the local type system is sound.
However, we can get more-precise guarantees, which we call
relative progress
and
relative preservation
.

Theorem 1

(Relative Preservation)
.

If the local type system enjoys
Preservation
, then for every choreography
C_{1}
such that
\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C_{1}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}
and
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
,

\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C_{2}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}
.

Theorem 2

(Relative Progress)
.

If the local type system enjoys
Boolean Invertability
and
Progress
, then for every choreography
C_{1}
such that
\cdot;\mkern 5.0mu\cdot\vdash C_{1}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}
, either
C_{1}
is a choreography value or there are some
R
,
B
, and
C_{2}
such that
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
.

Corollary 1

(Relative Soundness)
.

If the local type system is sound, as defined in Section
2.4
, then the choreographic type system enjoys progress and preservation.

By dividing up the result into more-precise theorems, we are able to get some guarantees even when the local type system is not sound.
For instance, the unityped
\lambda
-calculus example (Example
5
) is not sound, but does guarantee preservation.
Thus, Theorem
1
allows us to lift preservation to the choreographic system.
In this case, we do not get choreographic progress, intuitively because we may get stuck when trying to evaluate a local expression, or when an if expression tries to discriminate on a non-boolean value.

4.
Equational Reasoning

Choreographies represent collections of programs running in parallel.
In order to represent these programs serially, we are forced to decide what behavior to write first.
For instance, consider the program

{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.2+3\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.3*4\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}.y;\mkern 5.0muC
.
The program represents
\ell_{1}
sending a message to
\ell_{2}
while
\ell_{3}
sends a message to
\ell_{4}
.
We could have just as well represented that program as

{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.3*4\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}.y;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.2+3\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC
.
Since these choreographies represent the same collection of programs, all of our constructs treat them the same way.
In prior work on choreographies, this fact is typically formalized using a separate notion of
equivalence
, which says when two choreographies represent the same collection of programs
(
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017a
;
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017b
;
Montesi 2013
;
Lanese
et al. 2013
)
.
Following that tradition, we define a notion of structural equivalence for Pirouette and study its properties.

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}}{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e_{2}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}.y;\mkern 5.0muC\end{array}\mathrel{\equiv}\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e_{2}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}.y;\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC\end{array}}\quad\textsc{SwapSendSend}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}}{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}};\mkern 5.0muC\end{array}\mathrel{\equiv}\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC\end{array}}\quad\textsc{SwapSendSync}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e_{2}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\end{array}\end{array}\mathrel{\equiv}\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e_{2}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\end{array}}\quad\textsc{SwapSendIf}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}}{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d^{\prime}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}};\mkern 5.0muC\end{array}\mathrel{\equiv}\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d^{\prime}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}};\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0muC\end{array}}\quad\textsc{SwapSyncSync}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}{\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\end{array}\end{array}\mathrel{\equiv}\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\end{array}}\quad\textsc{SwapSyncIf}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}{\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.e_{2}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\end{array}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.e_{2}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{3}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{4}\end{array}\end{array}\mathrel{\equiv}\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.e_{2}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{3}\end{array}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{4}\end{array}\end{array}}\quad\textsc{SwapIfIf}

Figure 5.
Selected Choreography Equivalence Rules

We define choreography equivalence as the smallest equivalence relation which is also a congruence and satisfies the rules in Figure
5
.
The complete formal definition can be found in Appendix
C
.

Choreography equivalence respects types, even for open programs:

Theorem 3

(Equivalence Respects Types)
.

If
\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C_{1}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}
and
C_{1}\mathrel{\equiv}C_{2}
, then
\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C_{2}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}

Interestingly, Theorem
3
has an outsized influence on our system model.
In particular, in order to prove that the rule
SwapSendIf
respects types, we need to know that expressions have unique types.
After all, if
e_{2}
is given type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}}
in the
true
branch, but type
{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}}
in the
false
branch, there might not be a type that we could assign to
e_{2}
that makes both branches type check.
Including a typing annotation on send statements might solve this problem.
However, this would require mixing the type system with the syntax of choreographies.
While this is a reasonable choice in many situations, here we choose to keep them separate, since that makes it easy to tell when our results rely on the type system and when they do not.

Finally, our operational semantics respects equivalence, allowing us to prove the following simulation theorem:

Theorem 4

(Operational Semantics Simulates Equivalence)
.

If
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
and
C_{1}\mathrel{\equiv}C_{1}^{\prime}
, then there is a
C_{2}^{\prime}
such that
C_{2}\mathrel{\equiv}C_{2}^{\prime}
and
C_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}^{\prime}
.

Equivalence can be used to define a new, seemingly simpler operational semantics for choreographies.
This new semantics, which we write
\Rightarrow_{\equiv}
, has no redices or block sets and it replaces internal steps from our semantics with the following rule:

\frac{C_{1}\mathrel{\equiv}C_{1}^{\prime}\quad C_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\Rightarrow_{\equiv}}C_{2}^{\prime}\quad C_{2}^{\prime}\mathrel{\equiv}C_{2}}{C_{1}\mathrel{\Rightarrow_{\equiv}}C_{2}}\quad\textsc{EquivStep}

We formalize this semantics in Appendix
A.4
.

In fact, much of the prior work on choreographies defined their operational semantics in precisely this way
(
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017a
;
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017b
;
Carbone
et al. 2014
;
Montesi 2013
;
Lanese
et al. 2013
)
.
While this new semantics is good for prior work, it is too weak for Pirouette, which allows reduction of local expressions.
To see why, consider the program

{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e_{2}
.
It should be possible for
\ell_{3}
to evaluate its return value, even though
\ell_{1}
has not yet sent its message to
\ell_{2}
.
However, under the equivalence-based semantics, there is no way to reduce
e_{2}
, since we cannot use an equivalence to bring it up to the top.

A similar problem appears for sends.
Consider the program

{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e_{2}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC
.
In this example, both
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{3}
are trying to send a message to
\ell_{2}
.
If
e_{2}
can be reduced further, then
\ell_{3}
ought to be able to reduce
e_{2}
while waiting for
\ell_{2}
to be ready to receive the second message.
However, in the equivalence-based semantics, we cannot use
SwapSendSend
to bring up the second send, since that would swap the order of
\ell_{2}
’s receives.

Finally, consider programs that contain function applications, such as

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\\
(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}X)\mkern 5.0mu({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}.e_{2}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}.y;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{4}}.y)\end{array}

This program allows
\ell_{1}
to send a message to
\ell_{2}
and then applies the identity function to another choreography.
Importantly, the argument choreography does not mention
\ell_{1}
or
\ell_{2}
.
Therefore,
\ell_{3}
should be able to reduce
e_{2}
before
\ell_{1}
completes its send.
However, it is not possible to do this in the equivalence-based definition mentioned before.

This makes reasoning with
\equiv
much less powerful here than in previous choreographic systems.
However, the difficulty is limited to the problems mentioned above.

Theorem 5

(Weak Semantics)
.

Let
\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{w}}}
be the relation obtained by modifying the relation
\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}
as follows:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e
can only reduce when the block set is empty,

•

messages can only be reduced when neither the sender nor the receiver are in the block set, and

•

in
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}let}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{\coloneqq}C_{1}\mathrel{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}in}}}C_{2}
and function application, subchoreographies can only reduce when the block set is empty.

(The semantics
\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{w}}}
is formalized in Appendix
A.3
.)
Then whenever
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{w}}}}C_{2}
,
C_{1}\mathrel{\Rightarrow_{\equiv}}C_{2}
.
Moreover, whenever
C_{1}\mathrel{\Rightarrow_{\equiv}}C_{2}
, there is a redex
R
and choreography
C_{2}^{\prime}
such that
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{w}}}}C_{2}^{\prime}
and
C_{2}\mathrel{\equiv}C_{2}^{\prime}
.

While this makes our connection to previous work more clear, we prefer to work with the
\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}
relation defined in Section
3
due to its extra power.

5.
Endpoint Projection

While Pirouette programs are designed to represent a collection of concurrently-executing programs, so far that has been a guiding intuition rather than a formal property.
In order to change that, we define the
endpoint projection
operation, which
extracts a program
for each location from a choreography, if it is possible to do so.
This extracted program is expressed in a language called the
control language
which features local execution and explicit constructs for message passing.
The extracted programs of all locations are composed in parallel.
Here, we explain the control language, then explain the extraction and finally show that the parallel composition of all extracted programs reflects and preserves the operational semantics of the choreography.

5.1.
The Control Language

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\ignorespaces\textrm{Control Expression}&E&::=&X\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}fun}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}l}}}\mkern 5.0muF(x)\mathrel{\coloneqq}E\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}fun}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}g}}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}E\,\mid\,E\mkern 5.0mue\,\mid\,E_{1}\mkern 5.0muE_{2}\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(\hskip 1.00006pt)}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}e\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}let}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}x\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}E_{1}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}in}}\mkern 5.0muE_{2}\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}send}}\mkern 5.0mue\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}to}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell};\mkern 5.0muE\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}receive}}\mkern 5.0mux\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}from}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell};\mkern 5.0muE\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}if}}\mkern 5.0mue\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}then}}\mkern 5.0muE_{1}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}else}}\mkern 5.0muE_{2}\,\mid\,\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choose}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}for}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell};\mkern 5.0muE\\
&&\,\mid&\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E_{2}\\
\textrm{Systems}&\Pi&::=&{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\triangleright}}E_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\parallel}}\cdots\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\parallel}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{n}}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\triangleright}}E_{n}\end{array}

Figure 6.
Control Language Syntax

Our control language (Figure
6
) is a concurrent
\lambda
-calculus where messages are values of local programs.
It is inspired both by work on process calculi and by concurrent ML.

Like with Pirouette, control programs have two types of variable: local variables and control variables.
We write control variables with capital letters, because they play a role similar to that played by choreography variables.
Local variables are the variables of local programs.
There are correspondingly two types of functions, local functions and global functions (written
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}fun}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}l}}}\mkern 5.0muF(x)\mathrel{\coloneqq}E
and
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}fun}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}g}}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}E
, respectively).
Note that because in the control language—unlike in Pirouette—every local program is at the same location, local substitution does not take location into account.

Control programs can return the result of evaluating a local program, which we write
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}e\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}
.
We can use the result of such a program in another program using the syntax
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}let}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}x\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}E_{1}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}in}}\mkern 5.0muE_{2}
.
However, unlike choreographies, control programs can also return the trivial value
( )
.
This is used for control programs that do not have a return value on them.
