Communication happens between control programs composed in parallel through explicit send and receive commands.
We will see later how parallel composition works, and how communication takes place.

There are two forms of branching in the control language.
“If” statements are standard, and are a sequential form of branching.
We also have
external choice
, which is a distributed form of branching.
The program
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E_{2}\mkern 5.0mu
represents allowing
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
to choose which branch to take:
E_{1}
, labeled
L
, or
E_{2}
, labeled
R
.
The syntax
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choose}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}for}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell};\mkern 5.0muE
represents telling
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
to take the branch labeled
{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}
.

We refer to the parallel composition of a control program for each location as a
system
(we use the symbol
\Pi
to refer to systems).
The notation
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\triangleright}}E
says that program
E
is running on the node
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
.
As can be seen in Figure
6
, a system is a finite parallel compositions of such
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\triangleright}}E
.

We often use the syntax
\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\parallel}}\limits_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}E_{\ell}
to refer to a system where
\mathfrak{L}
is a finite set of locations and
E_{-}
is a function from locations to control program expressions.

Location semantics

In defining the operational semantics of systems, two syntactic operations will be useful.
The first is
system lookup
(written
\Pi({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{A}})
), which refers to the control program bound to a particular location
A
.
The second is
system update
(written
\Pi[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{A}}\mapsto E]
), which replaces the program bound to
A
.
We define them as follows:

\left(\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\parallel}}\limits_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathfrak{L}}E_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\right)({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{A}})=E_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{A}}}\left(\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\parallel}}\limits_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathfrak{L}}E_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\right)[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{A}}\mapsto E]=\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\parallel}}\limits_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathfrak{L}}E_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}^{\prime}~\text{where}~E_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}E&{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\textsf{A}}\\
E_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\ignorespaces\textrm{Label}&l&::=&\iota\,\mid\,v\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}:\mkern 5.0muv\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{}\,\mid\,[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}:\mkern 5.0mu[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\,\mid\,\iota_{\text{sync}}\,\mid\,\textsf{Fun}(l)\,\mid\,\textsf{Arg}(l)\end{array}

\frac{e_{1}\mathrel{\Rightarrow_{\textrm{e}}}e_{2}}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}send}}\mkern 5.0mue_{1}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}to}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell};\mkern 5.0muE\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\iota}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}send}}\mkern 5.0mue_{2}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}to}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell};\mkern 5.0muE}\quad\textsc{SendE}\\
\frac{\text{Value}(v)}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}send}}\mkern 5.0muv\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}to}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell};\mkern 5.0mux\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}from}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell};\mkern 5.0muE\mathrel{\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}:\mkern 5.0muv\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E[x\mapsto v]}\quad\textsc{RecvV}\frac{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choose}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}for}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell};\mkern 5.0muE\mathrel{\xRightarrow{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E}\quad\textsc{Choose}\frac{}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E_{2}\mkern 5.0mu\mathrel{\xRightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}:\mkern 5.0mu[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}]}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{1}}\quad\textsc{AllowChoiceL}\frac{\text{Value}(v)}{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}let}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}x\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}v\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\mkern 5.0muv\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\iota_{\text{sync}}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E[x\mapsto 5.0muV\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\iota_{\text{sync}}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E[X\mapsto V]}\quad\textsc{AppGlobal}

Figure 7.
Control Programs Semantics (Selected Rules)

The semantics of control programs is given via a labeled transition system.
This allows systems to match up corresponding rules in their semantics.
The syntax of labels and selected rules can be found in Figure
7
, and the full set of rules can be found in Appendix
D
.

Internal steps that do not interact with the outside are given the label
\iota
1
1

1

It is standard to use
\tau
to refer to internal steps.
However,
\tau
already represents Pirouette types.
We thus use
\iota
for “internal.”
.
For instance, the rule
SendE
takes a local step in a message to be sent to
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
, which is an internal step.
Every step of a local program corresponds to an
\iota
step.

Sends and receives are labeled with matching labels:
v\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
for sends, and
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}:\mkern 5.0muv\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{}
for receives.
Note that from the perspective of a single control-language program, receives are treated nondeterministically—any value could be received.
Our system semantics will force sends and receives to match up.
Similarly, external choice and its resolution have matching labels:
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}:\mkern 5.0mu[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]
for external choice and
[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
for its resolution.

In choreographies, all participants must
\beta
-reduce function applications together.
For instance, consider reducing a local function:

(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C)\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.e\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\text{LocalFun}({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.v)\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern-6.0mu\mid\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto v][F\mapsto\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C]

Because the choreography steps from a choreography with a function application to one without,
every
location’s control program changes.
In order to accommodate this, we use a new label,
\iota_{\text{sync}}
.
The only three rules labeled with
\iota_{\text{sync}}
are
LetRet
,
AppLocal
, and
AppGlobal
, all of which can be found in Figure
7
.

\begin{array}[]{llcl}\ignorespaces\textrm{System Label}&L&::=&\iota\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.v\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\,\mid\,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\,\mid\,\iota_{\text{sync}}\end{array}

Label Merge

\frac{}{\iota\mathrel{\bowtie_{\iota}}\iota}\quad\textsc{MergeIota}\frac{}{\iota_{\text{sync}}\mathrel{\bowtie_{\iota_{\text{sync}}}}\iota_{\text{sync}}}\quad\textsc{MergeSync}\frac{}{v\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\mathrel{\bowtie_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.v\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}:\mkern 5.0muv\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{}}\quad\textsc{MergeComm}\frac{}{[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\mathrel{\bowtie_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}:\mkern 5.0mu[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]}\quad\textsc{MergeChoice}\frac{l_{1}\mathrel{\bowtie_{L}}l_{2}}{\textsf{Fun}(l_{1})\mathrel{\bowtie_{L}}\textsf{Fun}(l_{2})}\quad\textsc{MergeFun}\frac{l_{1}\mathrel{\bowtie_{L}}l_{2}}{\textsf{Arg}(l_{1})\mathrel{\bowtie_{L}}\textsf{Arg}(l_{2})}\quad\textsc{MergeArg}

System Steps

\frac{\Pi({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell})\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\iota}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E}{\Pi\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\iota}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\Pi[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mapsto E]}\quad\textsc{Internal}\frac{l\mathrel{\bowtie_{\iota_{\text{sync}}}}l\quad\forall{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathfrak{L},~E_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}}{\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\parallel}}\limits_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathfrak{L}}E_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\iota_{\text{sync}}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\parallel}}\limits_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathfrak{L}}E_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{Synchronized Internal}\\

Figure 8.
System Semantics

System semantics

Systems are also given semantics via a labeled transition system.
The system labels arise from a merging operator on control-language labels, where
l_{1}\mathrel{\bowtie_{L}}l_{2}
ensures that labels
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
match, producing an output system label
L
.
It also ensures that, in a function application, either both steps reduce the function or both reduce its argument.
The syntax of system labels, label merge operator, and system semantics can all be found in Figure
8
.

The labels
\iota
and
\iota_{\text{sync}}
both refer to internal steps of a production.
Hence, they can be merged with themselves to yield the corresponding system label.
The rule
Internal
allows any location
\ell
to take an internal step without interfering with any other location.
Synchronized steps require the use of the
Synchronized Internal
rule, which requires that
every
location take a
\iota_{\text{sync}}
step.

Send and receive labels must be matched together.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
sends
v
to
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
, their labels merge together to a system label
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.v\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
in rule
MergeComm
.
The rule
Comm
then allows both
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
to take their corresponding steps together, without interfering with any other locations.
The
Choice
rule behaves similarly, but for choice-based branching.

5.2.
Merging Control Programs

Our goal is to extract a control program for every location in a choreography
compositionally
.
However, compositionality is greatly complicated by if branches.
To see why, consider the following choreography:

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.3\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.x;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.0\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.3\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.x;\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.1\end{array}

Intuitively, we want
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
to be associated with the control program

\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}send}}\mkern 5.0mu3\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}to}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}};\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}0\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}1\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\mkern 5.0mu

However, when defining our procedure formally, we want to extract a program for each branch of the if expression, and combine them together to get the final program.

This leads to two issues.
First, we need to be able to define a program for each branch.
Second, we need to be able to merge those two programs into a single program.

To see why it is difficult to define a program for each branch, consider the true branch of the program above.
We know that
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
will send a synchronization message to
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
.
Thus,
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
must allow
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}
to make a choice for it, as we saw earlier.
However, here we only have the
L
branch available; the
R
branch will not be available until we merge.

To solve this problem, we add one-branch choice constructs to our control language:

\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E\end{array}\hskip 30.00005pt\text{and}\hskip 31.0001pt\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E\end{array}

These act precisely like the two-choice construct, except that they only allow their one branch to be taken.
With these, we now have a program for
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}
we intend to extract from the branch above:

\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}send}}\mkern 5.0mu3\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}to}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}};\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}0\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}

\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\end{array}\right)\mathrel{\sqcup}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{2}\end{array}\right)\triangleq\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2}\end{array}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\end{array}\right)\mathrel{\sqcup}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E_{2}\end{array}\right)\triangleq\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E_{2}\\
\end{array}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\end{array}\right)\mathrel{\sqcup}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{2,1}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E_{2,2}\\
\end{array}\right)\triangleq\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2,1}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E_{2,2}\\
\end{array}

Figure 9.
Merge Operator Definition (Selected Parts)

We now need a way to merge the extracted programs from each branch into a single program.
We define a
partial
merge operator
\sqcup
, which ensures that two programs are the same until they allow both choices.
We adopt notation from computability theory and write
f(x)\uparrow
when
f
is a partial function to denote that the function is undefined.
So we would write
E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2}\uparrow
if the merge of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
is undefined.
We further write
f(x)\downarrow
(so
E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2}\downarrow
) if
f(x)
is defined, but we do not care about the value.
In Figure
9
, you can find the definition of the merge operator when the left-hand side is
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}
.
The remaining parts of the definition can be found in Appendix
E
.
Additional properties can be found in Appendix
G.3
.

5.3.
Endpoint Projection, Defined

We are now ready to define endpoint projection, or EPP.
Merging is an important part of the definition of EPP, and EPP inherits partiality from merging.
We continue to use
f(x)\uparrow
if
f(x)
is undefined and
f(x)\downarrow
if
f(x)
is defined, but we do not care about the value.

Endpoint projection is defined as follows:

\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}e\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(\hskip 1.00006pt)}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket X\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=X\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.x;\mkern 5.0muC\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\uparrow&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}send}}\mkern 5.0mue\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}to}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}receive}}\mkern 5.0mux\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}from}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}};\mkern 5.0mu\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0muC_{1}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}if}}\mkern 5.0mue\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}then}}\mkern 5.0mu\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}else}}\mkern 5.0mu\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}\mathrel{\sqcup}\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0muC\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\uparrow&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choose}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}for}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}~\text{and}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}={\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\mathrel{|}{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}~\text{and}~{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}={\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\\
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}let}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.x\mathrel{\coloneqq}C_{1}\mathrel{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}in}}}C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}let}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}x\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\mathrel{\coloneqq}\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}in}}\mkern 5.0mu\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
(\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}fun}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}g}}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}})\mkern 5.0mu\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}&\text{where $F,X$ are fresh, otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.x)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}fun}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}l}}}\mkern 5.0muF(x)\mathrel{\coloneqq}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}fun}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}g}}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}&\text{where $X$ is fresh, otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket C\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}\mkern 5.0mue&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(\hskip 1.00006pt)}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}fun}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}fun}}_{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}g}}}\mkern 5.0muF(X)\mathrel{\coloneqq}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\left\llbracket C_{1}\mkern 5.0muC_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\mkern 5.0mu\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}

Note a simple design principle: local expressions owned by a location other than the one being projected to are projected to
( )
.
This allows us to define a control program with the same control flow, but which does not know about the precise local expressions held at other locations.

This definition tells us what the control program for a single location is.
However, we are interested in a system of control programs.
We can lift the single-location definition to a multi-location system definition:

\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}=\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\parallel}}\limits_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathfrak{L}}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}\triangleright}}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}
.

One way of thinking about
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}
is that it gives
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
’s view of
C
.
From this perspective, it makes sense to ask what
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
’s view of a step of computation is.
We can provide this by projecting a choreography redex to a control-language label, as follows:

\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(e_{1}\Rightarrow e_{2})\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\iota&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(e_{1}\Rightarrow e_{2})\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\iota&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}true}}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\iota&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}false}}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\iota&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket\text{LocalArg}({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(e_{1}\Rightarrow e_{2}))\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\iota&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\\
\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(e_{1}\Rightarrow e_{2})\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tau&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(v\mkern 3.0mu\text{value})\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}v\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}:\mkern 5.0muv\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}:\mkern 5.0mu[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{3}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\uparrow&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}let}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\mathrel{\coloneq}(v\mkern 3.0mu\text{value})\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\iota_{\text{sync}}\left\llbracket\text{LocalFun}({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.v)\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\iota_{\text{sync}}\left\llbracket\text{GlobalFun}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=\iota_{\text{sync}}\left\llbracket\text{Arg}(R)\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=\textsf{Arg}(\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}})\left\llbracket\text{Fun}(R)\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=\textsf{Fun}(\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}})

Just as we can ask what some location’s view of a step of computation is, we can ask what a system’s view of a step of computation is.
Interestingly, this does not rely on which locations are in the system, and it is therefore a total operation.

\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(e_{1}\Rightarrow e_{2})\right\rrbracket=\iota\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(e_{1}\Rightarrow e_{2})\right\rrbracket=\iota\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}true}}\right\rrbracket=\iota\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}false}}\right\rrbracket=\iota\left\llbracket\text{LocalArg}({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(e_{1}\Rightarrow e_{2}))\right\rrbracket=\iota\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(e_{1}\Rightarrow e_{2})\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\right\rrbracket=\iota\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.(v\mkern 3.0mu\text{value})\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\right\rrbracket={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.v\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\right\rrbracket={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\left\llbracket\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}let}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\mathrel{\coloneq}(v\mkern 3.0mu\text{value})\right\rrbracket=\iota_{\text{sync}}\left\llbracket\text{LocalFun}({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.v)\right\rrbracket=\iota_{\text{sync}}\left\llbracket\text{GlobalFun}\right\rrbracket=\iota_{\text{sync}}\left\llbracket\text{Arg}(R)\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket\left\llbracket\text{Fun}(R)\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket

5.4.
Properties of Endpoint Projection

EPP is one of the most-important operations on choreographies.
It is what gives them a ground-truth interpretation as a parallel composition of programs.
In fact, without EPP it would be almost impossible to state one of our most-important theorems: deadlock freedom by construction (Theorem
12
below).

The first property we examine is how EPP treats equivalence.
As with every other operation on choreographies, we would like it if EPP treated equivalent choreographies the same.
Note that we have no notion of equivalence on control-language programs, so we get a strong notion of “treating the same”:

Theorem 6

(Equivalence Begets Equality)
.

If
C_{1}\mathrel{\equiv}C_{2}
, then
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathcal{L}
.

Next we would like to examine the relationship between the semantics of a choreography and the semantics of its projection.
However, there is still one remaining disconnect between the semantics of choreographies and that of systems that comes into play.
Yet again, it has to do with the semantics of external choice.
To see the issue, consider the following example:

C_{1}\triangleq\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}true}}\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}then}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.0\\
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}else}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.1\end{array}C_{2}\triangleq{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}]\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.0\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}0\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}1\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\\
\end{array}\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}ret}}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}(}}0\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72})}}\end{array}C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}if}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.58,0.11}true}}\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}

As you can see, by taking a choreography step which corresponds to a completely internal step on
\ell_{1}
, we have lost information about a possible path on
\ell_{2}
.
This comes because the choice of the path is up to
\ell_{1}
, who “makes up their mind” in that internal step.

One way to view this is from
\ell_{2}
’s point of view.
In the program
C_{1}
,
\ell_{2}
has a nondeterministic program: a message will come in to tell them which of two branches to take.
This is evident in the semantics of the control language, since
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}
can take either of two steps.
However,
C_{1}
is deterministic from
\ell_{2}
’s point of view, since only one message is possible.
Hence, this step has resolved some nondeterminism.

We formalize this notion of “the same program, but with some nondeterminism resolved” in a new relation called
\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}
.
This is
nearly
defined as the smallest relation that commutes with all of the control-language constructs and also fulfills the following extra rules:

\frac{E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2,1}}{\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\end{array}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{2,1}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E_{2,2}\\
\end{array}}\frac{E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2,2}}{\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E_{1}\end{array}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\begin{array}[]{l}\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}allow}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern 5.0mu\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.78,0,0.72}choice}}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{L}}\Rightarrow E_{2,1}\\
|\mkern 2.0mu{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}\textsf{R}}\Rightarrow E_{2,2}\\
\end{array}}

However, there is a small complication: functions are only related to themselves.
The full definition can be found in Appendix
F
.
The relation
\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}
is a partial order.

We extend the
\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}
relation to systems pointwise, so
\Pi_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\Pi_{2}
if for every
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
such that
\Pi_{1}({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell})\downarrow
,
\Pi_{2}({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell})\downarrow
and
\Pi_{1}({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell})\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\Pi_{2}({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell})
.
The following theorem relates
\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}
to the semantics of systems.
It may look complicated, but is not.
Its two bullet points respectively say: (1) If a less nondeterministic system takes a step, then that step is available to any more-nondeterministic system, and
(2) If a more nondeterministic system takes a step but the less nondeterministic system is the result of projecting some choreography, then we can take advantage of the fact that choices are always paired in choreographies to mimic the step in the less nondeterministic system.

Theorem 7

(Lifting and Lowering System Steps Across
\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}
)
.

If
\Pi_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\Pi_{2}
, then the following are both true:

•

If
\Pi_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{L}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\Pi_{1}^{\prime}
, then there is a
\Pi_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\Pi_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\Pi_{2}^{\prime}
and
\Pi_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{L}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\Pi_{2}^{\prime}
.

•

If
\Pi_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{L}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\Pi_{2}^{\prime}
and
\Pi_{1}=\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}
, then there is a
\Pi_{1}^{\prime}
such that
\Pi_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\Pi_{2}^{\prime}
and
\Pi_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{L}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\Pi_{1}^{\prime}
.

Finally, the less-nondeterminism relation allows us to connect the semantics of choreographies with the semantics of our control language.
Steps in the choreographies correspond to actions by one or more locations.
If a step involves a location, then that location’s control program also takes a step; the label of that step is the projection of the redex of the choreographic step.
However, if a step does not involve a location, then that location’s control program does not take a step and the projection of the redex for that location is undefined.
While an uninvolved location does not take a step, it may find its nondeterminism reduced as other locations “make up their minds” about what branch it should take in the future.

Theorem 8

(Local Completeness)
.

If
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
, then for any location
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}
, either (a)
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}
, or (b)
\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\uparrow
and
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}
.

Theorem 9

(Global Completeness)
.

If
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}B}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
and every location named in
R
is in
\mathfrak{L}
, then there is a
\Pi
such that
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\Pi
and
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\Pi
.

The requirement that every location named in
R
is in
\mathfrak{L}
is important: if
R
represents
\ell_{1}
sending a message to
\ell_{2}
, but
\ell_{2}
is not in
\mathfrak{L}
, then there is no one in
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}
to do the receiving.
This blocks
\ell_{1}
from sending its message (since message passing is synchronous) and thus blocks the system step from taking place.
However, if both
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are in
\mathfrak{L}
, then Theorem
8
tells us that
\ell_{2}
is guaranteed to be ready to receive
\ell_{1}
’s message.

A similar difficulty comes when trying to go in the other direction: it is not enough to know that
\ell_{1}
’s projection sends a message for a choreography to take a step; we must also know that
\ell_{2}
’s projection receives it.
This means that we have to state local soundness differently depending on the label of the control-program step:

Theorem 10

(Local Soundness)
.

All of the following are true:

•

If
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E
where
l\mathrel{\bowtie_{\iota}}l
, then there are
R
and
C_{2}
such that
\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=l
,
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=E
, and
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
.

•

If
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l_{1}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{1}
and
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l_{2}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{2}
where
l_{1}\mathrel{\bowtie_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.v\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}}l_{2}
, then there are
R
and
C_{2}
such that
(a)
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}=E_{1}
,
(b)
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=E_{2}
,
(c)
\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}=l_{1}
,
(d)
\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=l_{2}
, and
(e)
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
.

•

If
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l_{1}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{1}
and
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l_{2}}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{2}
where
l_{1}\mathrel{\bowtie_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}]\longrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}}l_{2}
, then there are
R
and
C_{2}
such that
(a)
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}=E_{1}
,
(b)
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=E_{2}
,
(c)
\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}=l_{1}
,
(d)
\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=l_{2}
, and
(e)
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
.

•

If
\textsf{LN}\left(C_{1}\right)\subseteq\mathfrak{L}\neq\emptyset
,
l\mathrel{\bowtie_{\iota_{\text{sync}}}}l
, and for every
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathfrak{L}

\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}
, then there are
R
and
C_{2}
such that
(a) for every
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathfrak{L}
,
\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=l_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}
, and
(b)
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}

The requirement that
\textsf{LN}\left(C_{1}\right)\subseteq\mathfrak{L}\neq\emptyset
may seem unusual in the last case of Theorem
10
.
In order to
\beta
-reduce a function call or a subexpression
\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}let}}\mkern 5.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{\coloneqq}C_{1}\mathrel{\textsf{{\color[rgb]{0.31,0.74,0}in}}}C_{2}
inside a choreography
C
, every location in
C
must be able to perform the same
\beta
-reduction.
However, if we only require that every location in
\textsf{LN}\left(C\right)
be able to make a step, the requirement might be trivial if
C
does not name any locations.
Therefore, we require that every location in some nonempty set of locations
\mathfrak{L}
which contains every location in
\textsf{LN}\left(C\right)
be able to make a step.
In the common case where
\textsf{LN}\left(C\right)
is nonempty, this restriction is the same as the simpler requirement.

Lifting soundness from control programs to systems yields a much simpler theorem.
However, note how the strange requirement for
\beta
-reduction steps infects the theorem:

Theorem 11

(Global Soundness)
.

If
\textsf{LN}\left(C_{1}\right)\subseteq\mathfrak{L}\neq\emptyset
and
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{L}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\Pi
then there is an
R
and
C_{2}
such that
(a)
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}\downarrow
,
(b)
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\Pi
,
(c)
\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket=L
, and
(d)
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
.

We use these to develop the most important theorem for Pirouette (and choreographies in general): deadlock freedom by design.
Interestingly, the proof is a simple interplay of soundness and completeness for our translation along with type soundness.

Theorem 12

(Deadlock Freedom By Design)
.

If choreography typing enjoys both progress and preservation (e.g., if local typing is sound),
\cdot;\mkern 5.0mu\cdot\vdash C_{1}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}
, and
\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{Ls}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}^{\ast}}\Pi
, then either every location in
\Pi
maps to a control-language value, or there are
L
and
\Pi^{\prime}
such that
\Pi\mathrel{\xRightarrow{L}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\Pi^{\prime}
.

Sketch of the mechanized proof.

By soundness, there are a list of redices
Rs
and a choreography
C_{2}
such that
C_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{Rs\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{2}
and
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}\Pi
.
By preservation,
\cdot;\mkern 5.0mu\cdot\vdash C_{2}\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau}
, and by progress either
C_{2}
is a choreography value or there are
R
and
C_{3}
such that
C_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{R\hfill{}\emptyset}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{c}}}}C_{3}
.
If
C_{2}
is a value, then every location in
\Pi
maps to a control-language value, and we are done.
Otherwise, completeness tells us there is a
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
such that
\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{L}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\Pi^{\prime\prime}
.
We can then use Theorem
7
to get a
\Pi^{\prime}
such that
\Pi\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\left\llbracket R\right\rrbracket}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{S}}}}\Pi^{\prime}
, as desired.
∎

6.
Notes on the Coq Code

In this paper, we have presented all of our work in a standard, named style.
However, in our Coq code we use a nameless style with de Bruijn indices.
While normally this would not pose any difficulties, we have taken the unusual step of treating the local language generically.
Here, we give the nameless version of our requirements on the local language, ensuring that the transition to the named style does not create undue confusion.

First, we require that expressions have decidable
syntactic
equality—usually a trivial requirement, since programs are usually sentences from a context-free grammar.
Next, we formalize the fact that variables must be expressions by requiring a mathematical function
var
from natural numbers to expressions.
In all of our examples, this is a terminal in the language, but this is not required.
Instead of the ability to compute the set of free variables of an expression, we require a predicate
\text{Closed}_{n}(e)
, which means that there are no free variables above
n
in
e
.
We require that
\text{Closed}_{n}(\text{var}(m))
if and only if
m<n
, and we write
\text{Closed}(e)
for
\text{Closed}_{0}(e)
.

Substitution is changed to allow for infinite parallel substitution.
Formally, we require an operation
e[\sigma]
where
\sigma
is a mathematical function from natural numbers to expressions.
This must obey the following equations:

•

\text{var}(n)[\sigma]=\sigma(n)

•

e[n\mapsto\text{var}(n)]=e

•

(e[\sigma_{1}])[\sigma_{2}]=e[n\mapsto(\sigma_{1}(n)[\sigma_{2}])]

•

if
\sigma_{1}(n)=\sigma_{2}(n)
for every natural number
n
, then
e[\sigma_{1}]=e[\sigma_{2}]

The last requirement is important because Coq is an
intensional
type theory, so functions that behave the same on all inputs are not necessarily equal.
The other three equations are nameless versions of the equations in Section
2
.

Finally, we require an additional
renaming
operation, which we write
e\langle\xi\rangle
, where
\xi
is a mathematical function from natural numbers to natural numbers.
This must satisfy the equation
e\langle\xi\rangle=e[n\mapsto\text{var}(\xi(n))]
.
While this equation could serve as a definition of renaming, it is often useful to define and reason about it separately.
For instance, we can use renaming to define exchange and weakening in typing.

Typing judgments in the nameless setting use contexts which are mathematical functions from natural numbers to types.
The requirements in Figure
1
are then equivalent to the following requirements:

\frac{}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\text{var}(n)\mathrel{:}\Gamma(n)}\quad\textsc{Var}\frac{\forall n,\Gamma(n)=\Delta(n)\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}}{\Delta\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}}\quad\textsc{Extensionality}\frac{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}}{\Gamma\circ\xi\mathrel{\vdash}e\langle\xi\rangle\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}}\quad\textsc{Weakening}\frac{\text{Closed}_{n}(e)\quad\forall m<n,\Gamma(m)=\Delta(m)\quad\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}}{\Delta\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}}\quad\textsc{Strengthening}\frac{\forall n,\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}\sigma(n)\mathrel{:}\Delta(n)\quad\Delta\mathrel{\vdash}e\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}}{\Gamma\mathrel{\vdash}e[\sigma]\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t}}\quad\textsc{Substitution}

Note that both
Exchange
and
Weakening
are absorbed into the more-general
Weakening
rule.
Moreover, we made
Substitution
infinitary.
The
Var
and
Strengthening
rules are straightforward transformations of the named versions in Figure
1
.

7.
Related Work

7.1.
Choreographies

Choreographies originate as a way of writing web services.
The W3C released a report on choreographies as a way to write web services
(
(W3C) 2004
)
, which originated the idea of endpoint projection
(
Zongyan
et al. 2007
)
(see
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017a
for the historical note).
Soon, there was work formalizing and understanding choreographies from this point of view.

Choreographic programming as a paradigm originates with Montesi’s Ph.D. thesis
(
Montesi 2013
)
.
There, he develops the first choreographic programming language and begins exploring the formal properties thereof.
Later works extend this idea, with
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017a
creating a core calculus of choreographies and
Carbone and
Montesi 2013
combining choreographies with session types to ensure that endpoint projection never fails.
However these works were all lower-order, used equivalence-based semantics, and had no mechanized metatheory.

More closely related with this project,
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017b
create a choreographic language with procedures and the ability to make procedure calls.
While parts of our endpoint projection definition are inspired by this work, procedural choreographies remain resolutely lower-order.
Procedures are global and shared, but cannot be treated as data; moreover, they cannot take other choreographies as inputs nor return them as outputs.
However, unlike our functions, these procedures are able to take locations as inputs.
This is harder in our setting because locations are part of types; we consider polymorphism (including location polymorphism) to be future work.

Some work has been done on higher-order choreographies.
The Choral Project
(
Giallorenzo et al. 2020
)
builds choreographies on top of object-oriented programming.
However, Choral has never been formalized, and so the theoretical underpinnings of higher-order choreographies were left unexplored.

Significantly, concurrent and completely independent work on functional choreographies has recently been undertaken by
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021a
.
This work resembles Pirouette in several ways, but has a significantly different design.
Most importantly, they identify the local language with the choreography language.
This design choice allows functions to be sent as messages, but they require that messages only mention a single location, preventing functions sent as messages from including communication.
It also leads to a simpler system, but makes it much harder for them to identify the core features of the local language which allow for type soundness of the choreographic language.
Moreover, their operational semantics allows for functions to be reduced; this allows them to not need as many synchronizations as Pirouette projection requires.
However, it violates the common requirement of call-by-value languages that functions be treated as values and makes execution more unpredictable.
We preferred to keep the simpler and more-traditional operational semantics.
Finally, they do not have any mechanized formalization of their system.

Mechanization

Two very recent papers have mechanized the metatheory of core choreographies, but both are lower-order.

Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021c
use Coq to formalize the core calculus of choreographies developed by
Cruz-Filipe and
Montesi 2017a
, modified so that the language of local computations is a parameter.
The focus is on proving that the language is Turing-complete.
In the meantime,
Cruz-Filipe et al. 2021b
extend that work to verify (in Coq) endpoint projection and deadlock freedom of the same language.

Interestingly, both these papers formalize out-of-order execution for choreographies using a labeled-transition system, similar to ours.
As far as we are aware, they, along with a forthcoming book on choreographies
(
Montesi 2020
)
were the first to do so, though our work was formalized before we discovered their concurrent work.
Their work takes inspiration from
Honda
et al. 2016
, who use a similar labeled-transition system semantics for multiparty session types.
Thus, they do not use any construct similar to our block sets.
This means that they cannot allow locations to locally reduce their messages when the receiver of that message is blocked.
While for their language this does not matter (since messages do not reduce at all), in our setting this would be problematic.

7.2.
Functional Concurrent Programming

There is a long tradition of mixing functional and concurrent programming in principled ways.
In practice, this often leads to languages that look a lot like our control language, including the concurrency features in Racket
(
The Racket Team 2021
)
.
The first academic language with channels and communication in a functional language was Facile
(
Giacalone
et al. 1989
)
.
Even before then, work on parallelizing compilers for functional programming was popular.

Burton 1987
considered adding annotations to functions describing when arguments should be evaluated at what location.

Currently, the most related academic project might be Links
(
Cooper
et al. 2006
)
and its core language, the RPC calculus
(
Cooper and Wadler 2009
)
.
Links also provides for a mixture of higher-order typed functional programming and concurrency.
However, in Links, communication always happens at function boundaries, unlike Pirouette.
Moreover, the RPC calculus only allows for one thread of execution, even when multiple unrelated locations can profitably be taking actions.
This is in contradiction to the work on choreographies—and Pirouette in particular—where unrelated locations can compute and even pass messages concurrently.

Another language which mixes functional and concurrent programming is
Murphy VII
et al. 2007
’s ML5
(
Licata and Harper 2010
;
Murphy VII
et al. 2007
)
.
ML5 has a type system for communication based on the Kripke semantics of modal logic.
Interestingly, our type system also takes inspiration from modal logic: our type system can be seen as (the Curry-Howard analogue of) a degenerate form of the
proof system
for modal logic.
Their use of the Kripke semantics makes sense, however, because different worlds can be viewed as different machines.
Unfortunately, this led to difficulties in shipping some data that could be treated as code, so ML5 focuses on annotating
mobile code
, which includes static types like strings and integers, but not functions or local resources like arrays.
This restriction is necessary due to their use of local state.
Since Pirouette has no state, is has no such constraint.

Appendices

Appendix A
Full Pirouette Operational Semantics

A.1.
Local Substitution, Defined

X[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern-6.0mu\mid\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto e]=X({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1})[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\mkern-6.0mu\mid\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto e_{2}]=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.e_{1}[x\mapsto e_{2}]&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\

Appendix G
Additional Theorems

G.1.
Additional Properties of the Pirouette Type System

While we do not explicitly supply the structural rules, our type system admits them:

Theorem 13

(Pirouette Types Structural Rules)
.

The choreographic type system admits weakening, exchange, and strengthening in both contexts.
In other words, the following rules are admissible:

\frac{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.y\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}},\Gamma^{\prime};\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{3}}}{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}.y\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}.x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}},\Gamma^{\prime};\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{3}}}\quad\textsc{Local Exchange}\frac{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta,X\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}},Y\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}},\Delta^{\prime}\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{3}}}{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta,Y\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}},X\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}},\Delta^{\prime}\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{3}}}\quad\textsc{Global Exchange}\\
\frac{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}}{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{2}};\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}}\quad\textsc{Local Weakening}\frac{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}}{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta,X\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}}\quad\textsc{Global Weakening}\\
\frac{\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}.x\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.05,0.81,0.82}t_{1}};\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}\quad x\notin\text{FEV}_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}(C)}{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}}\quad\textsc{Local Strengthening}\frac{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta,X\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{1}}\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}\quad X\notin\text{FCV}(C)}{\Gamma;\mkern 5.0mu\Delta\vdash C\mathrel{:}{\color[rgb]{0.61,0.09,0.94}\tau_{2}}}\quad\textsc{Global Strengthening}

G.2.
Additional Properties of Choreography Equivalence

Our syntactic operations respect choreography equivalence:

Theorem 14

(Equivalence of Syntactic Operations)
.

If
C_{1}\mathrel{\equiv}C_{1}^{\prime}
and
C_{2}\mathrel{\equiv}C_{2}^{\prime}
, then all of the following statements are true:

•

\text{FEV}(C_{1})=\text{FEV}(C_{1}^{\prime})

•

FCV(C_{1})=\text{FCV}(C_{1}^{\prime})

•

\textsf{LN}\left(C_{1}\right)=\textsf{LN}\left(C_{1}^{\prime}\right)

•

if
C_{1}
is a value, then so is
C_{1}^{\prime}

•

C_{1}[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern-6.0mu\mid\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto e]\mathrel{\equiv}C_{1}^{\prime}[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\mkern-6.0mu\mid\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto e]

•

C_{1}[X\mapsto C_{2}]\mathrel{\equiv}C_{1}^{\prime}[X\mapsto C_{2}^{\prime}]

G.3.
Additional Properties of Merging

Theorem 15
.

The following are true for all
E
,
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
, and
E_{3}
:

•

E\mathrel{\sqcup}E=E

•

E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2}=E_{2}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{1}

•

(E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2})\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{3}=E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}(E_{2}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{3})

Theorem 16
.

The following are true for all control-language expressions
E
,
E_{1}
, and
E_{2}
, along with all control-language
values

V
.

•

\text{FV}(E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2})=\text{FV}(E_{1})\cup\text{FV}(E_{2})

•

\text{FEV}(E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2})=\text{FEV}(E_{1})\cup\text{FEV}(E_{2})

•

(E_{1}[x\mapsto e])\mathrel{\sqcup}(E_{2}[x\mapsto e])=(E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2})[x\mapsto e]

•

(E_{1}[X\mapsto E])\mathrel{\sqcup}(E_{2}[X\mapsto E])=(E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2})[X\mapsto E]

•

Either
(V\mathrel{\sqcup}E)\uparrow
or
E=V=V\mathrel{\sqcup}E=E\mathrel{\sqcup}V

•

If
E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2}=V
, then
E_{1}=E_{2}=V

Theorem 17
.

If
E_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{L}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{1}^{\prime}
,
E_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{L}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{2}^{\prime}
, and
(E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2})\downarrow
, then
(E_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2}^{\prime})\downarrow
and
E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{L}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2}^{\prime}
.

G.4.
Additional Properties of Endpoint Projection

Our second property tells us that projection plays nicely with all of the syntactic operations that we have defined on choreographies:

Theorem 18
.

All of the following are true:

•

\text{FEV}_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}(C)=\text{FEV}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}})

•

\text{FCV}(C)=\text{FV}(\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}})

•

\left\llbracket C_{1}[X\mapsto C_{2}]\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}=\left\llbracket C_{1}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}[X\mapsto\left\llbracket C_{2}\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}]

•

\left\llbracket C[{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}\mkern-6.0mu\mid\mkern 2.0mux\mapsto e]\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}[x\mapsto e]&\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{2}}\\
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell_{1}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

•

If
V
is a value, then so is
\left\llbracket V\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}
.

•

If
\left\llbracket C\right\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}}
is a value for every
{\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}\in\mathcal{L}
, then
C
is a value.

G.5.
Additional Properties of
\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}

Theorem 19

(
\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}
is a Partial Order)
.

The following are all true for all
E
,
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
, and
E_{3}
.

•

E\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E

•

if
E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}
and
E_{2}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{1}
, then
E_{1}=E_{2}

•

if
E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}
and
E_{2}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{3}
, then
E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{3}

Theorem 20
.

The following are all true for all choices of
E_{i}
.

•

If
E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}
, then
(E_{1}[x\mapsto e])\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}(E_{2}[x\mapsto e])

•

If
E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}
, then
(E_{1}[X\mapsto E_{3}])\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}(E_{2}[X\mapsto E_{3}])

•

E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2}
and
E_{2}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{2}

•

If
E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}
and
E_{3}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{4}
and
(E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{3})\downarrow
then
(E_{2}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{4})\downarrow
and
E_{1}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{3}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}\mathrel{\sqcup}E_{4}

•

If
E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}
and either of
E_{1}
or
E_{2}
is a value, then
E_{1}=E_{2}
.

Theorem 21

(Steps Lift Across
\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}
)
.

If
E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}
and
E_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{1}^{\prime}
, then there is a
E_{2}^{\prime}
such that
E_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}^{\prime}
and
E_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{2}^{\prime}
.

When a more-nondeterministic expression takes a step, it may be taking advantage of the extra nondeterminism to take a step not available to the original.
We can take advantage of the fact that our operational semantics is defined via a labeled-transition system to lift steps which do not resolve nondeterminism.
In order to turn this into a theorem, we need to determine when a label may reduce an external choice.
We do this with the predicate
\text{Choice}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}}(l)
, which we define as follows:

\frac{}{\text{Choice}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}}({\color[rgb]{0.85,0.52,0}\ell}:\mkern 5.0mu[{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}])}\frac{\text{Choice}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}}(l)}{\text{Choice}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}}(\textsf{Fun}(l))}\frac{\text{Choice}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}}(l)}{\text{Choice}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}}(\textsf{Arg}(l))}

With the definition of
\text{Choice}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.18,0.83}d}}(-)
in hand, we can prove the following theorem:

Theorem 22

(Lowering Steps Across
\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}
)
.

If
E_{1}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}
and
E_{2}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{2}^{\prime}
where
\lnot\text{Choice}_{d}(l)
, then there is a
E_{1}^{\prime}
such that
E_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{\leq_{\text{nd}}}E_{2}^{\prime}
and
E_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{l}_{\raisebox{3.0pt}{\footnotesize{E}}}}E_{1}^{\prime}
.
